Let's define:
- adj[i] bitmask of neighbors.
- dom[i] 3-bit mask (bit 0 for color1, bit1 color2, bit2 color3). Initially 0b111 or precolored bit.
- forced if dom[i] & (dom[i]-1) == 0 and dom[i]!=0.

Propagation:
```
queue = [i for i in range(n) if forced]
while queue:
  u = pop
  c = bit index of dom[u]
  mask = adj[u] & vertices_not_yet? 
  for v in neighbors:
     if dom[v] has bit c:
        dom[v] &= ~(1<<c)
        if dom[v]==0: fail
        if forced: queue.append(v)
```

- If graph disconnected, 3-coloring constraints independent. We can check each connected component separately. But domains may have precolored vertices. But for lexicographic construction, we can decompose globally and solve each component independently, as argued. This could greatly reduce. Let's consider implementing a function `solve_component(vertices)` that returns lexicographically smallest coloring for that component (with global indices) or None. Then combine. Let's prove independence: Given graph components C1..Ck. For any assignment, constraints only within components. Lexicographically smallest global assignment: For vertex 1 (smallest index), its color only constrained by its component. To minimize c1, choose smallest color c such that there exists a coloring of its component with vertex1 color c. Other components can be colored arbitrarily later. So we can fix c1 minimal for that component. Then proceed to next vertex; if same component, continue; if different, its component independent. Thus global lexicographically smallest is obtained by independently computing lexicographically smallest coloring of each component (with respect to the order of vertices in that component? Wait if components interleave, the lexicographic order of global sequence is not simply concatenation of component sequences sorted by component. But because no cross constraints, at each global index i, the color of vertex i is determined solely by its component's partial assignment. The choices for vertices in other components with smaller indices have already been fixed. Since they don't affect feasibility of vertex i, the minimal color for vertex i is the minimal color for that vertex given its component's previous assignments.     # But if a vertex is forced and processed, its dom remains singleton; if we process it again in same propagate, okay but avoid duplicates.

    while q:
        u = q.pop()
        inq[u]=False
        c = bit_index[dom[u]]  # 0,1,2
        bit = 1<<c
        # for each neighbor v of u:

                if dom[v] == 0:
                    return False
                if dom[v] & (dom[v]-1) == 0 and not inq[v]:

Implementation:
```
def can_extend(dom):
    dom = dom.copy()
    if not propagate(dom): return False
    failed = set()
    def dfs(dom_tuple):
        if dom_tuple in failed: return False
        dom = list(dom_tuple)
        # choose vertex
        best = -1; best_size = 4
        for i in range(n):
            s = dom[i].bit_count()
            if s > 1 and s < best_size:
                best_size = s; best = i
                if s == 2: break? maybe still check tie
        if best == -1:
            return True  # all singleton
        # branch
        # optional symmetry: if no singleton? But if best_size==3 and no singleton? We can detect if all domains are 3? Then fix best to color 1? But if there are domains size 2, no.
        colors = [c for c in range(3) if dom[best] & (1<<c)]
        # maybe order colors by some heuristic
        for c in colors:
            new_dom = dom.copy()
            new_dom[best] = 1<<c
            if propagate(new_dom):
                if dfs(tuple(new_dom)):
                    return True
        failed.add(dom_tuple)
        return False
    return dfs(tuple(dom))
```

    # adj list length n
    dom = [0b111]*n
    ans = [0]*n
    # We need ensure initial propagate? no forced.
    for i in range(n):
        # dom[i] may be singleton due to previous assignments/propgation
        if pop[dom[i]] == 1:
            c = bit_to_idx[dom[i]]
            ans[i] = c+1
            # no need propagate? Actually if dom[i] singleton, its color already propagated to neighbors in current dom. But if we didn't call propagate after previous assignment? We will call propagate after each assignment and update dom. So yes.
            continue

        for c in range(3):
            if dom[i] & (1<<c):
                new_dom = dom.copy()
                new_dom[i] = 1<<c
                if propagate(new_dom, adj) and can_extend(new_dom, adj):
                    ans[i] = c+1
                    dom = new_dom
                    break
        else:

    return ans
```

Now, can_extend implementation details.

Let's write `propagate(dom, adj)`:
```
def propagate(dom, adj):
    n = len(dom)
    q = [i for i in range(n) if dom[i] and (dom[i] & (dom[i]-1)) == 0]
    while q:
        u = q.pop()
        c = bit_to_idx[dom[u]]
        bit = 1 << c
        for v in adj[u]:
            if dom[v] & bit:
                dom[v] &= ~bit
                if dom[v] == 0:
                    return False
                if (dom[v] & (dom[v]-1)) == 0:
                    q.append(v)
    return True
```
But if q initialized with all forced vertices, and a forced vertex u is processed, it may remove its color from neighbor v. If v becomes forced, append. Maybe use bitmask propagation to be faster. Let's consider.

Represent domains as list of 3-bit masks. Propagation can be done with bit operations over adjacency masks:
- For each color c, maintain `allowed[c]` bitmask of vertices that can use color c. But domains list is enough.
- When a vertex u is forced to color c, we need clear bit c from all neighbors. Instead of iterating neighbors, we can compute `neighbors_mask = adj_mask[u]` and `to_clear = neighbors_mask & allowed[c]` (where allowed[c] is bitmask of vertices whose dom has bit c). Then for each v in to_clear, clear bit c. But still need iterate over set bits to update dom and detect new forced. Number of cleared vertices maybe less than degree. We can maintain `allowed_mask[c]` as bitmask of vertices that still allow color c. Initially all vertices. When dom[v] loses color c, clear bit v in allowed_mask[c]. Then if dom[v] becomes singleton, process v. This can speed.

But we also need choose MRV variable; can compute popcount from dom.

Let's design a more optimized `propagate` using bitmasks:
- `dom` list length n.
- `allowed` list length 3: bitmask of vertices that have color c in domain.
- `forced` bitmask? We can compute queue of forced vertices.

Function `propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed)` modifies dom and allowed. But allowed can be derived from dom: allowed[c] = bitmask of vertices where dom[v] & (1<<c). We can compute initially O(n). Then when clearing, update.

Pseudo:
```
def propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
    # allowed[c] bitmask of vertices that still allow color c
    q = [i for i in range(n) if singleton]
    while q:
        u = q.pop()
        c = bit_to_idx[dom[u]]
        bit = 1<<c
        # neighbors that still allow color c
        affected = adj_mask[u] & allowed[c]
        # clear bit c from those vertices
        while affected:
            lsb = affected & -affected
            v = lsb.bit_length()-1
            affected -= lsb
            dom[v] &= ~bit
            allowed[c] &= ~lsb
            if dom[v] == 0:
                return False
            if singleton(dom[v]):
                q.append(v)
    return True
```
Need update allowed for other colors? When dom[v] loses color c, only allowed[c] changes. Good.
But if v becomes singleton, its color is some d; it will be processed later. If v was already singleton before? It couldn't be in affected because if v was singleton {d}, and u forced c. If c==d, then dom[v] & bit true, clearing would make 0 -> fail. If c!=d, dom[v] & bit false, so v not in affected. Thus affected vertices are not singleton before clearing (or if they are singleton of same color, fail). So no duplicate q.

This is faster. Need maintain `allowed` in can_extend and solve_component. But can_extend state can be represented by dom tuple; allowed can be recomputed from dom at start of dfs. To avoid recomputing each state, we can pass allowed? But for memoization, dom tuple enough; allowed derived. In dfs, we can compute allowed from dom list at beginning. That's O(n). Fine.

However, in recursive branching, we copy dom list and allowed list? We can copy dom and recompute allowed after propagation? Let's design can_extend with dom tuple and inside convert to list, compute allowed, propagate, then branch. For each branch, create new_dom = dom.copy(), new_allowed = allowed.copy()? But if we use propagate with allowed, we need allowed consistent with new_dom. We can copy allowed too. Or recompute allowed from new_dom after setting color before propagate? Actually after setting dom[best]=bit, allowed needs update: for colors other than c, clear best from allowed; for color c, best remains. We can update allowed manually:
```
new_dom = dom.copy()
new_allowed = allowed.copy()
old = dom[best]
for col in range(3):
    if old & (1<<col) and col != c:
        new_allowed[col] &= ~(1<<best)
new_dom[best] = 1<<c
# allowed[c] already has best if old had c; if old didn't have c? We only branch colors in dom, so old has c. So allowed[c] includes best.
```
Then propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed). This avoids recompute.

But for memoization, we only need dom tuple. We can store failed dom tuples. In dfs, we can compute allowed from dom at start. That's okay.

Now, in can_extend dfs:
```
def can_extend(dom, adj_mask):
    dom = dom.copy()
    allowed = [0,0,0]
    for i,d in enumerate(dom):
        if d & 1: allowed[0] |= 1<<i
        if d & 2: allowed[1] |= 1<<i
        if d & 4: allowed[2] |= 1<<i
    if not propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed): return False
    failed = set()
    def dfs(dom_tuple):
        if dom_tuple in failed: return False
        dom = list(dom_tuple)
        # compute allowed? We can compute from dom each time.
        allowed = [0,0,0]
        for i,d in enumerate(dom):
            if d & 1: allowed[0] |= 1<<i
            if d & 2: allowed[1] |= 1<<i
            if d & 4: allowed[2] |= 1<<i
        # choose best
        best = -1; best_size = 4; best_deg = -1
        for i in range(n):
            s = pop[dom[i]]
            if s > 1:
                if s < best_size or (s == best_size and deg[i] > best_deg):
                    best_size = s; best = i; best_deg = deg[i]
                    if s == 2:
                        # maybe still check tie? We can break if s==2 and deg high? Not necessarily.
                        pass
        if best == -1:
            return True
        # branch
        colors = []
        d = dom[best]
        for c in range(3):
            if d & (1<<c): colors.append(c)
        # Optional: if no singleton domains and best_size==3? color symmetry? We'll handle later.
        for c in colors:
            new_dom = dom.copy()
            new_allowed = allowed.copy()
            # remove best from other colors
            for col in range(3):
                if col != c and (d & (1<<col)):
                    new_allowed[col] &= ~(1<<best)
            new_dom[best] = 1<<c
            # allowed[c] already has best because d has c
            if propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                if dfs(tuple(new_dom)):
                    return True
        failed.add(dom_tuple)
        return False
    return dfs(tuple(dom))
```

def compute_allowed(dom):
    allowed = [0,0,0]

def propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
    q = [i for i in range(n) if dom[i] and (dom[i] & (dom[i]-1)) == 0]
    while q:
        u = q.pop()
        c = bit_to_idx[dom[u]]
        bit = 1 << c
        affected = adj_mask[u] & allowed[c]
        while affected:
            lsb = affected & -affected
            v = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            affected ^= lsb
            dom[v] &= ~bit
            allowed[c] &= ~lsb
            if dom[v] == 0:
                return False
            if (dom[v] & (dom[v]-1)) == 0:
                q.append(v)
    return True
```

Now, solve_component_local:
```
def solve_component_local(n, adj, adj_mask, deg):
    dom = [0b111]*n
    ans = [0]*n
    failed_memo = set()
    for i in range(n):
        if pop[dom[i]] == 1:
            ans[i] = bit_to_idx[dom[i]] + 1
            continue
        # try colors
        chosen = False
        for c in range(3):
            if dom[i] & (1<<c):
                new_dom = dom.copy()
                new_dom[i] = 1<<c
                # Need propagate and update allowed? We can call can_extend which will propagate. But we also need dom for next iterations to include propagation. We can create new_allowed and propagate here, then if can_extend true set dom = new_dom.
                new_allowed = compute_allowed(new_dom)  # or from dom
                # Actually compute_allowed(new_dom) before propagation.
                if propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                    if can_extend(new_dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
                        ans[i] = c+1
                        dom = new_dom
                        chosen = True
                        break
        if not chosen:
            return None
    return ans
```

Also, if propagate succeeds but can_extend returns False, can_extend will add states to failed_memo. Good.

Now, can_extend's dfs uses `failed_memo` and may add many states. But if we call can_extend with a dom that is already propagated, and it returns False, it will add original dom_tuple to failed_memo. Good.

Let's refine can_extend to avoid double propagation and to use failed_memo correctly.

```
def can_extend(dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
    dom_tuple = tuple(dom)
    if dom_tuple in failed_memo:
        return False
    dom = list(dom_tuple)
    allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
    if not propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
        failed_memo.add(dom_tuple)
        return False
    # Now dom is propagated. We should use tuple(dom) as state for dfs.
    return dfs(tuple(dom))
```
But if dom was already propagated, propagate will process all forced vertices again. Fine.

In dfs, we need `n = len(dom)`. We can capture from outer.

```
def dfs(dom_tuple):
    if dom_tuple in failed_memo:
        return False
    dom = list(dom_tuple)
    allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
    # choose best
    best = -1
    best_s = 5
    best_deg = -1
    for i in range(n):
        s = pop[dom[i]]
        if s > 1:
            if s < best_s or (s == best_s and deg[i] > best_deg):
                best_s = s; best = i; best_deg = deg[i]
    if best == -1:
        return True
    d = dom[best]
    # branch colors
    for c in range(3):
        if d & (1<<c):
            new_dom = dom.copy()
            new_allowed = allowed.copy()
            # remove best from other colors
            for col in range(3):
                if col != c and (d & (1<<col)):
                    new_allowed[col] &= ~(1<<best)
            new_dom[best] = 1<<c
            if not propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                failed_memo.add(tuple(new_dom))
                continue
            if dfs(tuple(new_dom)):
                return True
    failed_memo.add(dom_tuple)
    return False
```
Need note: `allowed` computed from dom before branching. When we set new_dom[best]=1<<c, we update new_allowed for other colors. But what about allowed[c]? Since d has bit c, allowed[c] includes best. We keep it. Good. If d did not have bit c, we wouldn't branch. Good.

But after propagate, `new_allowed` may be modified. We don't need it further except for deeper branches? In dfs, we recompute allowed from dom_tuple at start, so no need to pass allowed down. Good.

Potential issue: In dfs, after propagate on new_dom, if it succeeds, we call dfs(tuple(new_dom)). But `new_dom` is a list; tuple is hashable. Good.

Now, can_extend's initial propagate may change dom. If it succeeds, we call dfs(tuple(dom)). But if dfs returns False, we add original dom_tuple to failed_memo. However, what if original dom_tuple is not propagated and dfs state is propagated; if original appears again, we will propagate again and then dfs will see propagated state in failed_memo and return False. So adding original is optional. We can add original to save propagation. But if original dom_tuple is already in failed_memo? We checked at start. Good.

Now, solve_component's greedy loop: We call can_extend(new_dom) after propagating new_dom. But can_extend will copy and propagate again. Could optimize by having can_extend accept already-propagated dom and skip initial propagate? But not necessary.

However, there is a potential issue with `failed_memo` and can_extend's initial propagate: Suppose a dom_tuple is in failed_memo because a more restrictive propagated state failed. If we call can_extend with a less restrictive dom that is not in failed_memo, it may propagate to the same failed state and return False. Good.

Now, let's think about correctness of greedy with can_extend. At each vertex i, we try colors in increasing order. For each color c, we check if there exists any completion of the current partial assignment (dom) with vertex i color c. If yes, we fix c. This is standard for lexicographically smallest. Need ensure `dom` before trying i represents all constraints from previous fixed vertices and propagation. Yes.

But wait: In solve_component, when we reach vertex i, `dom[i]` may have been reduced by propagation from previous vertices. If `dom[i]` is singleton, we set ans[i] to that color without checking can_extend. Is it possible that this forced color leads to no completion, even though previous can_extend said there was a completion? No, because previous can_extend ensured there exists a completion with the previous choices. Propagation is sound: any completion must respect reduced domains. If dom[i] singleton, any completion must use that color. Since a completion exists, it's fine. So no need can_extend.

Now, component decomposition proof: We need be absolutely sure. Let's formalize: Graph G has connected components C1..Ck. The constraint set is union of disjoint constraints. For any assignment, feasibility is conjunction over components. The lexicographic order over all vertices: Let vertices sorted by index. For vertex v in component C, its color does not affect feasibility of any vertex in other components. When constructing lexicographically smallest, at step v, the colors of earlier vertices in other components are already fixed. They impose no constraints on v. The only constraints on v are from earlier vertices in the same component (and later vertices in same component, but existence check handles them). Thus the minimal color for v depends only on its component's partial assignment. Therefore the global lexicographically smallest assignment is obtained by, for each component independently, assigning its vertices in increasing index order with the same greedy rule. Since components don't interact, the order in which we solve components doesn't matter. Good.

Now, what about isolated vertices? Each is a component of size 1, ans=1. Good.

Now, need handle input m=0. Components all isolated. solve_component for each size1 returns [1]. Output YES and all 1s. Lexicographically smallest all 1. Good.

Encoding: For each vertex i, dom[i] is 0..7. We can encode as `key |= dom[i] << (3*i)`. 3*40=120 bits. Python int handles. Decoding: `dom[i] = (key >> (3*i)) & 7`. This is O(n) per state. def can_extend(dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
    key = bytes(dom)
    if key in failed_memo:
        return False
    dom = list(dom)
    allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
    if not propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
        failed_memo.add(key)
        return False
    # after propagation, dom may changed; use new key
    return dfs(bytes(dom))
```

Let's implement `compute_allowed(dom)`:
```
def compute_allowed(dom):
    allowed = [0,0,0]
    for i, d in enumerate(dom):
        if d & 1:
            allowed[0] |= 1 << i
        if d & 2:
            allowed[1] |= 1 << i
        if d & 4:
            allowed[2] |= 1 << i
    return allowed
```

Propagate with `n = len(dom)`.

Now, can_extend's initial propagate: If dom is already propagated, it will process all forced vertices. But with bytes key, if propagate changes dom, the key changes. We call dfs on propagated key. If dfs returns False, we add original key. Good.

Now, solve_component's greedy loop with failed_memo:
```
failed_memo = set()
for i in range(n):
    if pop[dom[i]] == 1:
        ans[i] = bit_to_idx[dom[i]] + 1
        continue
    for c in range(3):
        if dom[i] & (1<<c):
            new_dom = dom.copy()
            new_dom[i] = 1<<c
            new_allowed = compute_allowed(new_dom)
            if not propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                failed_memo.add(bytes(new_dom))
                continue
            if can_extend(new_dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
                ans[i] = c+1
                dom = new_dom
                chosen = True
                break
    if not chosen: return None
```

def has_triangle_in_neighborhood(n, adj, adj_mask):
    for v in range(n):
        nbrs = adj[v]
        # if len(nbrs) < 3 continue
        for a_idx in range(len(nbrs)):
            a = nbrs[a_idx]
            mask_a = adj_mask[a]
            for b in nbrs[a_idx+1:]:
                if mask_a & (1<<b):
                    return True
    return False
```

Now, let's consider if the graph has multiple components and we solve them independently. We need fill global ans. We'll have `ans = [0]*n_global`. For each component, get local colors list, then for local index i, original vertex `verts[i]`, set `ans[verts[i]] = colors[i]`. Now, let's write the full code structure.

```
import sys

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()
    if not data: return
    it = iter(data)
    n = int(next(it)); m = int(next(it))
    adj_global = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(m):
        u = int(next(it))-1; v = int(next(it))-1
        adj_global[u].append(v)
        adj_global[v].append(u)
    # find components
    visited = [False]*n
    comps = []
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            stack=[i]; visited[i]=True; comp=[]
            while stack:
                u=stack.pop(); comp.append(u)
                for v in adj_global[u]:
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v]=True; stack.append(v)
            comps.append(comp)
    ans = [0]*n
    for comp in comps:
        verts = sorted(comp)
        k = len(verts)
        idx = {v:i for i,v in enumerate(verts)}
        adj = [[] for _ in range(k)]
        adj_mask = [0]*k
        for i,v in enumerate(verts):
            for w in adj_global[v]:
                j = idx[w]
                adj[i].append(j)
                adj_mask[i] |= 1<<j
        deg = [len(a) for a in adj]
        colors = solve_component_local(k, adj, adj_mask, deg)
        if colors is None:
            print("NO")
            return
        for i,v in enumerate(verts):
            ans[v] = colors[i]
    print("YES")
    print(" ".join(map(str, ans)))
```

Need define `solve_component_local`.

Global constants:
```
pop = [0,1,1,2,1,2,2,3]
bit_to_idx = {1:0, 2:1, 4:2}
```

Functions:
```
def compute_allowed(dom):
    allowed = [0,0,0]
    for i,d in enumerate(dom):
        if d & 1:
            allowed[0] |= 1 << i
        if d & 2:
            allowed[1] |= 1 << i
        if d & 4:
            allowed[2] |= 1 << i
    return allowed
```

`propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed)`:
```
def propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
    n = len(dom)
    q = [i for i in range(n) if dom[i] and (dom[i] & (dom[i]-1)) == 0]
    while q:
        u = q.pop()
        c = bit_to_idx[dom[u]]
        bit = 1 << c
        affected = adj_mask[u] & allowed[c]
        while affected:
            lsb = affected & -affected
            v = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            affected ^= lsb
            dom[v] &= ~bit
            allowed[c] &= ~lsb
            if dom[v] == 0:
                return False
            if (dom[v] & (dom[v]-1)) == 0:
                q.append(v)
    return True
```
Need `bit_to_idx` accessible. Since dom[u] singleton, value 1,2,4. Use dict.

`can_extend(dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo)`:
```
def can_extend(dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
    key = bytes(dom)
    if key in failed_memo:
        return False
    dom = list(dom)
    allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
    if not propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
        failed_memo.add(key)
        return False
    def dfs(key):
        if key in failed_memo:
            return False
        dom = list(key)
        allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
        n = len(dom)
        best = -1
        best_s = 5
        best_deg = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if deg[i] <= 2:
                continue
            s = pop[dom[i]]
            if s > 1:
                if s < best_s or (s == best_s and deg[i] > best_deg):
                    best_s = s
                    best = i
                    best_deg = deg[i]
        if best == -1:
            return True
        d = dom[best]
        for c in range(3):
            if d & (1 << c):
                new_dom = dom.copy()
                new_allowed = allowed.copy()
                # remove best from other colors
                for col in range(3):
                    if col != c and (d & (1 << col)):
                        new_allowed[col] &= ~(1 << best)
                new_dom[best] = 1 << c
                if not propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                    failed_memo.add(bytes(new_dom))
                    continue
                if dfs(bytes(new_dom)):
                    return True
        failed_memo.add(key)
        return False
    res = dfs(bytes(dom))
    if not res:
        failed_memo.add(key)
    return res
```
Wait: In dfs, we compute `allowed` from `dom` (which is already propagated). But when we branch, we copy `allowed`. However, `allowed` corresponds to `dom` before setting best. We update for other colors. But what about if `dom[best]` has bit c, allowed[c] includes best. Good. If `dom[best]` has other colors, we remove best from those allowed. Good.

But there is a subtle issue: `allowed` computed from `dom` after propagation. If `dom` has some forced vertices, allowed reflects their colors. Good.

Now, in `can_extend`, after initial propagate, `dom` may have changed. We call `dfs(bytes(dom))`. But `dfs` will check if key in failed_memo. If the propagated key was already failed from a previous call, it returns False. Good.

Potential issue: In `dfs`, when we add `failed_memo.add(key)` at the end, `key` is the propagated state. But if the same state is reached again, it returns False. Good.

Now, `solve_component_local`:
```
def solve_component_local(n, adj, adj_mask, deg):
    # precheck triangle in neighborhood
    for v in range(n):
        nbrs = adj[v]
        for i in range(len(nbrs)):
            a = nbrs[i]
            mask_a = adj_mask[a]
            for j in range(i+1, len(nbrs)):
                b = nbrs[j]
                if mask_a & (1 << b):
                    return None
    dom = [0b111] * n
    ans = [0] * n
    failed_memo = set()
    for i in range(n):
        if dom[i] == 0:
            return None
        if pop[dom[i]] == 1:
            ans[i] = bit_to_idx[dom[i]] + 1
            continue
        chosen = False
        for c in range(3):
            if dom[i] & (1 << c):
                new_dom = dom.copy()
                new_dom[i] = 1 << c
                new_allowed = compute_allowed(new_dom)
                if not propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                    failed_memo.add(bytes(new_dom))
                    continue
                if can_extend(new_dom, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
                    ans[i] = c + 1
                    dom = new_dom
                    chosen = True
                    break
        if not chosen:
            return None
    return ans
```
But note: In the loop, if `dom[i]` is singleton, we set ans and continue. But we don't update `dom` (it's already propagated). However, what if `dom[i]` became singleton due to propagation from a previous assignment, and that propagation was performed when we set `dom = new_dom`. Yes. But what if `dom[i]` was singleton initially because of precheck? No precolored. So okay.

But there is a subtle bug: When we try a color for i and `can_extend` returns True, we set `dom = new_dom`. But `new_dom` was propagated before calling can_extend. However, `can_extend` may have modified `new_dom`? We pass `new_dom` list to can_extend. In can_extend, we do `dom = list(dom)` at start, so it doesn't modify the passed list. Good. So `new_dom` remains propagated. Good.

Now, what about if `dom[i]` is singleton and we continue, but later a vertex j < i? We process in increasing i, so no later changes to earlier. Good.

But adj_mask[a] & adj_mask[b] won't include a or b (since no self), so common excludes a,b. Good. So:
```
for v in range(n):
    nbrs = adj[v]

```
def has_triangle_in_neighborhood(n, adj, adj_mask):
    for v in range(n):
        nbrs = adj[v]
        for i in range(len(nbrs)):
            a = nbrs[i]
            for j in range(i+1, len(nbrs)):
                b = nbrs[j]
                if adj_mask[a] & (1 << b):
                    if adj_mask[a] & adj_mask[b] & adj_mask[v]:
                        return True
    return False
```
But wait: If a-b edge and common = adj_mask[a] & adj_mask[b] & adj_mask[v] nonzero, then there is a vertex c adjacent to a,b,v. But is c necessarily distinct from a and b? adj_mask[a] doesn't include a, adj_mask[b] doesn't include b, so yes. Is c a neighbor of v? adj_mask[v] includes c. So triangle a,b,c in N(v). Good.

Complexity: For each v, for each pair of neighbors that are adjacent, one bitwise AND. In dense graph, many pairs. K40: for each v, C(39,2)=741 pairs, all adjacent, bitwise AND. 40*741 ~ 30k, fine. Actually C(39,2)=741, not 9139. Good.

Now, let's think about the lexicographic greedy with can_extend that ignores deg<=2. Suppose at vertex i, we try color c and can_extend returns True because it ignores a deg<=2 vertex that actually cannot be colored with the current partial assignment due to a subtle list issue. We argued safe. Let's try to find a concrete reachable counterexample to be absolutely sure.

We need a graph with a vertex v of degree <=2, and a partial assignment (domains) such that:
- Propagation leaves all domains nonempty.
- The subgraph induced by vertices of degree >2 (hard) has a coloring.
- But v cannot be colored in any extension.

Since v has at most 2 neighbors, say a,b. For v to be uncolorable, in every coloring of a,b, the colors used by a,b cover all colors in D(v). Since D(v) nonempty.

Case |D(v)|=1, D={c}. Then a,b must use colors other than c? Actually v cannot use c if a or b uses c. To make v uncolorable, at least one of a,b must use c in every coloring. That means a or b is forced to c. If a is forced to c, propagation would have removed c from v (because edge a-v), so D(v) wouldn't contain c. Contradiction. If a is not forced but every coloring uses c? If a has domain {c} only, forced. If a has domain {c,d}, there is a coloring with d, so not every. Thus impossible.

Case |D(v)|=2, D={c,d}. Missing color e. To make v uncolorable, in every coloring, a and b together use both c and d (so no c or d left). Since e is missing from D(v), some neighbor is forced to e. If every vertex has at most 2 allowed colors, the problem is a binary CSP with inequality constraints. This is equivalent to 2-SAT and can be solved in polynomial time. We can implement a simple algorithm:
- For each vertex, if domain size 1, it's fixed.
- For each edge (u,v), the forbidden combinations are (u=c and v=c) for each color c that is in both domains.

This translates to clauses on x_u,x_v depending on colors. This is a 2-SAT instance. We can solve with implication graph. n<=40, easy.

Alternative: For 2-coloring with lists, we can use BFS with "color equality/inequality" constraints. Since each vertex has at most 2 colors, but the two colors may not be the same pair. If u has colors {a,b}, v has {c,d}. The forbidden equalities are for colors in intersection. This is a binary constraint. It can be represented as 2-SAT.

Let's derive 2-SAT:
For each vertex i with domain size 2, let colors (x_i, y_i). Define boolean variable b_i: if False choose x_i, if True choose y_i. For a vertex with domain size 1, no variable (fixed).
For each edge (u,v):
- If both have domain size 2:
  Let u colors (a,b), v colors (c,d). For each color col in intersection of {a,b} and {c,d}, we need forbid (u=col and v=col).
  This gives clauses:
    (b_u != (col==b? True:False)) OR (b_v != (col==d? True:False))? Actually if u=col means b_u = (col == b) (since a is False, b is True). Similarly v. Forbid both true: clause (¬(b_u == val_u)) ∨ (¬(b_v == val_v)) = (b_u != val_u) OR (b_v != val_v). This is a 2-CNF clause.
- If one domain size 1, say u fixed color col. Then v cannot be col. If v's domain contains col, remove it; if becomes empty fail. This is propagation.
- If both domain size 1, if same color fail.

We can implement 2-SAT with n variables <=40, clauses <= edges*3. Then check satisfiability with SCC. This is polynomial and fast.

2-SAT implementation:
- Variables indexed 0..k-1 for vertices with domain size 2. We need map vertex -> var index.
- For each vertex with domain size 2, store `colors0[i]`, `colors1[i]` (the two colors, maybe sorted). Variable False -> colors0, True -> colors1.
- For each edge (u,v):
   - If both have domain size 2:
       for col in intersection of domains:
           val_u = (col == colors1[u])  # True if variable True gives col
           val_v = (col == colors1[v])
           # forbid (var_u == val_u AND var_v == val_v)
           # clause: (var_u != val_u) OR (var_v != val_v)
           # In CNF: (¬var_u OR ¬var_v) if val_u=val_v=True; etc.
           Add implications: (var_u == val_u) -> (var_v != val_v); (var_v == val_v) -> (var_u != val_u).
   - If one domain size 1:
       fixed color col. If other domain contains col, remove it (propagation should have done, but we can check). If other domain becomes empty fail. If other domain size 2, this forces variable to the other color. We can add unit clause.
   - If both domain size 1: if same fail.
- Also, if a vertex domain size 2 but one color is impossible due to unit clauses, 2-SAT handles.

But note: Domains may have size 2 but the two colors could be any pair. We need map.

2-SAT graph: 2*k nodes (False, True). Add implication edges. Then check SCC: for each var, var False and var True not in same SCC.

Let's implement `solve_2color(dom, adj)`:
```
def solve_2color(dom, adj):
    n = len(dom)
    # First, propagate fixed colors? The dom should already be propagated, but we can handle.
    var_id = [-1]*n
    colors = [[-1,-1] for _ in range(n)]
    k = 0
    for i in range(n):
        d = dom[i]
        if d == 0: return False
        if pop[d] == 2:
            # find two bits
            bits = []
            for c in range(3):
                if d & (1<<c): bits.append(c)
            var_id[i] = k
            colors[i] = bits  # [color0, color1]
            k += 1
    # Build implication graph
    N = 2*k
    g = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    def add_imp(a, va, b, vb):
        # (var a == va) -> (var b == vb)
        # node index: var*2 + (1 if True else 0)? Let's define node for literal (var, val) as var*2 + val (val 0 False, 1 True).
        g[a*2 + va].append(b*2 + vb)
    def add_clause(var1, val1, var2, val2):
        # forbid (var1==val1 and var2==val2)
        # (var1 != val1) OR (var2 != val2)
        # implications: var1==val1 -> var2!=val2; var2==val2 -> var1!=val1
        add_imp(var1, val1, var2, 1-val2)
        add_imp(var2, val2, var1, 1-val1)
    for u in range(n):
        du = dom[u]
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v < u: continue? To avoid double, but okay.
            dv = dom[v]
            if du == 0 or dv == 0: return False
            if pop[du] == 1 and pop[dv] == 1:
                if du == dv: return False
            elif pop[du] == 1:
                col = bit_to_idx[du]
                if dv & (1<<col):
                    # v cannot col; if dv becomes singleton? But dom is fixed; if dv has exactly col and another? Actually if dv contains col, removing col leaves one color if size2, or 0 if size1.
                    new_dv = dv & ~(1<<col)
                    if new_dv == 0: return False
                    if pop[new_dv] == 1:
                        # force v to that color
                        # add unit clause: var_v == (new_dv == colors[v][1])
                        vv = var_id[v]
                        val = 1 if new_dv == colors[v][1] else 0
                        # unit clause: var_v must be val. Add implication var_v != val -> var_v == val? Standard: add_imp(vv, 1-val, vv, val)
                        add_imp(vv, 1-val, vv, val)
                    # if new_dv size 2, no constraint? Actually removing one color from a 2-color domain leaves singleton, so size2 -> size1. So always singleton.
                # if dv doesn't contain col, no constraint
            elif pop[dv] == 1:
                col = bit_to_idx[dv]
                if du & (1<<col):
                    new_du = du & ~(1<<col)
                    if new_du == 0: return False
                    vu = var_id[u]
                    val = 1 if new_du == colors[u][1] else 0
                    add_imp(vu, 1-val, vu, val)
            else:
                # both size 2
                # intersection of colors
                for col in range(3):
                    if (du & (1<<col)) and (dv & (1<<col)):
                        vu = var_id[u]; vv = var_id[v]
                        val_u = 1 if colors[u][1] == col else 0
                        val_v = 1 if colors[v][1] == col else 0
                        add_clause(vu, val_u, vv, val_v)
    # SCC (Kosaraju or iterative Tarjan)
    # n vars <=40, nodes <=80, simple.
    ...
```
But wait: If a vertex has domain size 2 and an edge to a fixed color removes one color, the domain becomes singleton. In our dom list, it's still size2 because we don't modify dom. But the constraint forces the variable to the remaining color. We add unit clause. Good.

    for v in g[u]:
        if not visited[v]: dfs(v)
    order.append(u)

    if not visited[i]: dfs(i)
comp = [-1]*N

    comp[u]=c
    for v in rg[u]:
        if comp[v]==-1: rdfs(v,c)
cid=0
for u in reversed(order):
    if comp[u]==-1:
        rdfs(u,cid); cid+=1
for i in range(k):
    if comp[2*i] == comp[2*i+1]:
        return False
return True
```

Now, let's implement solve_2color.

Detailed implementation:

```
def solve_2color(dom, adj):
    n = len(dom)
    var_id = [-1]*n
    colors = [[-1,-1] for _ in range(n)]
    k = 0
    for i in range(n):
        d = dom[i]
        if d == 0:
            return False
        if pop[d] == 2:
            # find two colors
            bits = []
            if d & 1: bits.append(0)
            if d & 2: bits.append(1)
            if d & 4: bits.append(2)
            var_id[i] = k
            colors[i] = bits  # [color0, color1]
            k += 1
    N = 2*k
    g = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    rg = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    def add_imp(a, va, b, vb):
        u = a*2 + va
        v = b*2 + vb
        g[u].append(v)
        rg[v].append(u)
    def add_clause(var1, val1, var2, val2):
        # forbid (var1==val1 and var2==val2)
        add_imp(var1, val1, var2, 1-val2)
        add_imp(var2, val2, var1, 1-val1)
    for u in range(n):
        du = dom[u]
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v < u:
                continue  # process each edge once
            dv = dom[v]
            if du == 0 or dv == 0:
                return False
            pu = pop[du]; pv = pop[dv]
            if pu == 1 and pv == 1:
                if du == dv:
                    return False
            elif pu == 1:
                col = bit_to_idx[du]
                if dv & (1 << col):
                    new_dv = dv & ~(1 << col)
                    if new_dv == 0:
                        return False
                    # new_dv must be singleton because pv==2
                    vv = var_id[v]
                    val = 1 if new_dv == colors[v][1] else 0
                    # force vv = val
                    add_imp(vv, 1-val, vv, val)
            elif pv == 1:
                col = bit_to_idx[dv]
                if du & (1 << col):
                    new_du = du & ~(1 << col)
                    if new_du == 0:
                        return False
                    vu = var_id[u]
                    val = 1 if new_du == colors[u][1] else 0
                    add_imp(vu, 1-val, vu, val)
            else:
                # both size 2
                for col in range(3):
                    if (du & (1 << col)) and (dv & (1 << col)):
                        vu = var_id[u]; vv = var_id[v]
                        val_u = 1 if colors[u][1] == col else 0
                        val_v = 1 if colors[v][1] == col else 0
                        add_clause(vu, val_u, vv, val_v)
    # SCC
    visited = [False]*N
    order = []
    def dfs(u):
        visited[u] = True
        for w in g[u]:
            if not visited[w]:
                dfs(w)
        order.append(u)
    for i in range(N):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs(i)
    comp = [-1]*N
    def rdfs(u, c):
        comp[u] = c
        for w in rg[u]:
            if comp[w] == -1:
                rdfs(w, c)
    cid = 0
    for u in reversed(order):
        if comp[u] == -1:
            rdfs(u, cid)
            cid += 1
    for i in range(k):
        if comp[2*i] == comp[2*i+1]:
            return False
    return True
```

Need ensure `colors[v][1]` exists for domain size2. Yes.

But wait: In the case `pu == 1` and `pv == 2`, if `dv` contains `col`, removing it leaves a singleton. But what if `dv` had size 2 and contained col, new_dv is the other color. We force v to that color. Good. If `dv` did not contain col, no constraint. If `dv` had size 1 and same as col, handled by both size1. If `dv` size1 different, no constraint.

Now, in the both size2 case, if du and dv share a color col, we add clause. But what if du and dv are the same domain {a,b} and edge, we add two clauses: forbid (u=a & v=a) and (u=b & v=b). This allows u=a,v=b and u=b,v=a. Good.

Now, what about a vertex with domain size 2 but one color is forced by multiple unit clauses; 2-SAT handles.

Now, is 2-SAT with variables per vertex sufficient for constraints "u and v cannot have same color" when domains size2? Yes, because each vertex chooses exactly one of its two colors. The clauses forbid same color assignments. Good.

```
def can_extend(dom, adj, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo):
    key = bytes(dom)
    if key in failed_memo:
        return False
    dom = list(dom)
    allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
    if not propagate(dom, adj_mask, allowed):
        failed_memo.add(key)
        return False
    def dfs(key):
        if key in failed_memo:
            return False
        dom = list(key)
        allowed = compute_allowed(dom)
        # Check if all domains <=2
        max_s = 0
        for d in dom:
            s = pop[d]
            if s > max_s:
                max_s = s
        if max_s <= 2:
            res = solve_2color(dom, adj)
            if not res:
                failed_memo.add(key)
            return res
        n = len(dom)
        best = -1
        best_s = 5
        best_deg = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if deg[i] <= 2:
                continue
            s = pop[dom[i]]
            if s > 1:
                if s < best_s or (s == best_s and deg[i] > best_deg):
                    best_s = s; best = i; best_deg = deg[i]
        if best == -1:
            return True
        d = dom[best]
        for c in range(3):
            if d & (1 << c):
                new_dom = dom.copy()
                new_allowed = allowed.copy()
                for col in range(3):
                    if col != c and (d & (1 << col)):
                        new_allowed[col] &= ~(1 << best)
                new_dom[best] = 1 << c
                orig_key = bytes(new_dom)
                if not propagate(new_dom, adj_mask, new_allowed):
                    failed_memo.add(orig_key)
                    continue
                if dfs(bytes(new_dom)):
                    return True
        failed_memo.add(key)
        return False
    res = dfs(bytes(dom))
    if not res:
        failed_memo.add(key)
    return res
```

But note: If max_s <=2 and solve_2color returns False, we add key and return False. But what if the state has no hard vertices and max_s<=2 but solve_2color returns False due to a deg<=2 list issue? That would mean our earlier assumption wrong, but solve_2color would catch. Good.

Now, in solve_component, when we call can_extend(new_dom, adj, adj_mask, deg, failed_memo), we need pass adj. Good.

    seen = set()

Now, let's think about if solve_2color's SCC correctly handles unit clause. Example force var=0: add_imp(var,1,var,0). Graph has edge from True node to False node. If var False, no issue. If var True, implies False, contradiction if SCC? In SCC, True and False not necessarily same SCC? Let's check: edge True->False. There is no edge False->True. So True and False not in same SCC, but assignment var=True would require True->False, meaning if True chosen, False must also be chosen? In 2-SAT, implication True->False means if var is True, then var must be False, contradiction. The SCC algorithm detects unsat if var True and var False in same SCC. But here they are not in same SCC, so would it incorrectly say satisfiable? Wait, unit clause forcing var=0 is represented by implication var=1 -> var=0. In 2-SAT, a clause (var=0) is (var=1 -> var=0) and also (var=0 -> var=0)? Actually unit clause (x) is equivalent to (¬x -> x). In implication graph, add edge ¬x -> x. That's it. If x is False, then ¬x is True, implying x True, contradiction. The SCC algorithm checks if x and ¬x in same SCC. Here edge ¬x -> x. There is no edge x -> ¬x. So x and ¬x are not in same SCC. But the formula with only clause (x) is satisfiable by x=True. Wait unit clause (x) means x must be True. The implication ¬x -> x is satisfied by x=True (¬x false, implication true). So satisfiable. If we want force var=0 (x=False), we add implication x -> ¬x (var=1 -> var=0). Then assignment x=True makes implication false? Actually if x=True, then x -> ¬x requires ¬x true, contradiction. The formula is satisfiable by x=False. SCC: edge x -> ¬x. x and ¬x not same SCC, so satisfiable. Good. So unit clause add_imp(vv, 1-val, vv, val) is correct. If val=0, add_imp(vv,1,vv,0) (x -> ¬x). Satisfiable by x=False. If val=1, add_imp(vv,0,vv,1) (¬x -> x). Satisfiable by x=True. Good.
